And I can see her lips moving, I can hear the kindness in her voice, but it's like... it's like I'm sitting at the very bottom of a deep, dark well, and she's way up at the top, calling down to me. The warmth of what she's saying just doesn't reach me down there. I can't feel it. I just sit there with that heavy emptiness, looking up at the light but completely unable to climb out. It's just this constant, overwhelming sadness that I can't seem to shake, no matter how hard I try or how much support I have around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble following conversations or train of thought in your daily interactions?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, exactly. That's... that's exactly what I was trying to explain earlier about the note for the kids. It's not just writing, it's conversations, it's my own train of thought... it all just evaporates. \n\nLike I was saying before, I sit down to do something so simple, like writing that little instruction note for the youth maker program. I mean, I've been doing that program for a while now, and before I retired, I managed complex projects, spreadsheets, endless meetings where I had to track a dozen different moving parts. I'm a college-educated man, I've always prided myself on my mental sharpness. But now? I sit there with the pen in my hand, and I think, \"Okay, I need to tell them to cut the balsa wood at a 45-degree angle.\" And before the ink even touches the paper, the thought is just... gone. It scatters. \n\nAnd it's the same when people talk to me. My wife will be telling me about her sister's upcoming visit, you know, going over the dates and what we need to get from the grocery store. She'll say, \"We need to get the guest room ready by Thursday,\" and I'll hear the words, I understand what Thursday is, I understand what a guest room is... but stringing it all together and holding onto the meaning? It just slips away. I find myself nodding along, but inside, I'm frantically trying to grab onto the pieces of the conversation, like trying to catch leaves blowing in the wind. The numbers, the words, the plans... they just scatter on me. It's like my mind just refuses to hold onto anything anymore, whether I'm trying to write it down or just listen to my own wife speak. It's so incredibly frustrating, doctor, to feel your own mind just refusing to work the way it always has.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disoriented or unsure of what time or day it is?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I know what day it is. I know it's Tuesday, I know it's morning, I know I'm sitting here in your office. It's not like I'm waking up thinking it's 1995 or anything like that. \n\nMy wife actually asked me something similar the other day. She came in while I was sitting in the armchair—the green one by the window, where I've been spending most of my time lately—and she asked if I knew what time we were supposed to leave for the pharmacy. And I knew it was 2:30. I knew it was Thursday. I just... I didn't care. Or, well, not that I didn't care, but it just felt like such an insurmountable task to get up and go. But the actual awareness of time and place? No, that's all still intact. \n\nIt's just the contents of my thoughts that are scrambled, you know? The dates on the calendar, the clock on the wall... those are fixed. Those stay still. It's everything else that's swirling around. I always know where I am and when it is. I just... I just don't know what to do with myself once I'm there. It's like I'm anchored to the calendar perfectly fine, but the boat itself is just filling with water. Does that make sense? I'm not confused about the day, I'm just... I'm just so bogged down in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering recent events or learning new information?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not so much that I forget things that just happened. I mean, if my wife tells me she's making chicken for dinner, I know we're having chicken. I don't forget that she said it. And I remember her bringing me my tea this morning, in the blue mug, the one our daughter bought us on her trip to Maine. I remember the steam rising off it and how it warmed my hands, even if it didn't do much to warm me on the inside. \n\nSo no, it's not like my memory is wiped clean of what happened yesterday or this morning. It's more about... well, like I was saying before, it's the holding onto the threads of things as they're happening. Learning new things? Gosh, I haven't even tried to learn anything new since... since my sister passed. The very thought of trying to sit down and read a book or figure out a new recipe or, heaven forbid, try to learn a new computer program or something... it just feels impossible. Not because my brain can't store the information, but because I can't even gather enough focus to let the information in in the first place. \n\nIt's like... imagine trying to fill a bucket with water, but the bucket has a lid on it. It's not that the bucket is broken or has holes in it, it's just that the water just splashes right off the top. That's how I feel when I try to take in anything new right now. The lid is just shut tight with all this... this heaviness. So I wouldn't say I have amnesia or anything like that. I remember the events of the day perfectly fine. I just... I just don't have the energy or the mental space to absorb anything new or complicated. It's all just too much effort. Everything is just too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble performing your daily routines or tasks due to these cognitive difficulties?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I am. And it's... it's a terrible feeling, to be honest with you. It's not just the note I was telling you about earlier, the one for the youth maker program. It's the whole program. I've had to just... let it go. I've been involved with those kids for years, teaching them how to build things, how to use their hands, how to take pride in a project from start to finish. And now? I can't even walk into my own workshop. \n\nMy wife and my pastor, Pastor Miller—he's a good man, he's known me for decades—they actually had to step in and take over because... well, because I just don't feel safe being out there by myself anymore. The tools, the machinery, the saws... when your mind is as scattered as mine is right now, when you can't hold onto a thought long enough to write a simple sentence, being around a table saw is a recipe for disaster. I just stare at the equipment and feel this overwhelming sense of dread, like I'm going to make a mistake that I can't undo. So I've had to completely step away from the program. \n\nAnd it's not just the maker program, either. It's bleeding into everything. I've been on the church property committee since, gosh, since the late nineties. I was always the one organizing the spring clean-up, making sure the gutters were cleared before the winter, coordinating the volunteers. I had spreadsheets for all of it. But I haven't been to a committee meeting in months. I just stopped going. I couldn't face the thought of sitting there, trying to follow the agenda, trying to make decisions about budgets and repairs when I can barely decide whether to get out of my chair in the morning. \n\nEven my garden... I've always loved my garden. Tomatoes, zucchini, a whole row of sunflowers that my grandchildren love to measure themselves against in the late summer. I usually start my seeds indoors in February, getting everything ready for the spring thaw. But this year? The seed packets are still sitting in a shoebox in the mudroom, unopened. The weeds are probably taking over the raised beds by now, but I haven't even gone out to look. I just sit in my chair and look out the window, and I know I should go out there, I know I should pull the weeds and turn the soil, but the thought of actually doing it... it's like trying to move a mountain. I just can't. So, yes, my daily routines, the things that used to make up my life, the things that gave me purpose and structure... they've all just fallen away. I'm not doing any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience a full range of emotions, or do you feel numb to things that would normally affect you?\n\nPatient: Numb... I don't know if numb is exactly the right word for it, because numb makes it sound like there's nothing there at all. Like novocaine at the dentist. But for me, it's not an absence of feeling, it's... well, it's the presence of something else that just blocks everything normal out. \n\nMost days, the very second I open my eyes—before I even fully register the sunlight coming through the blinds or hear the birds outside—I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling right in the center of my chest. It's a strange contradiction, isn't it? To feel so incredibly heavy and yet completely hollow at the exact same time. But it's like a lead weight that's just empty inside, pressing down on my ribs before I've even thrown off the covers. \n\nAnd because that feeling is so huge, so all-consuming, there just isn't room for anything else. No joy, no excitement, not even regular sadness. Just that heavy ache. And it puts this... this terrible distance between me and the rest of the world. Even with my wife. She's been so incredibly patient through all of this, more than I deserve, really. She'll come into the room, and she'll talk so kindly to me, speaking softly, trying to offer some comfort or just tell me about her morning. A husband should feel that, right? He should feel the warmth of his wife's voice. \n\nBut when she's standing right there, being so gentle and loving... I just feel like I'm sitting at the very bottom of a deep, dark well. I can look up and see the little circle of light at the top, and I can hear her voice echoing down to me, but she's so far away. The kindness just doesn't reach me down there in the dark. I know in my head that she loves me, but I can't feel the comfort of it. I can't feel the connection. I'm just stuck down at the bottom of the well with that hollow weight in my chest. So, no, I'm definitely not experiencing a full range of emotions. It's just that one, constant, heavy emptiness swallowing up everything else that tries to get in.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain people, places, or activities because they remind you of your sister?\n\nPatient: Well... no. No, I wouldn't say that's it. I mean, it's true that I'm avoiding practically everything and everyone right now, like I told you about the workshop and the garden and the church committee. But it's not... it's not because those things specifically remind me of her. \n\nShe lived a couple of states away, you see. So it's not as if my daily routine around town is full of places we used to go together, where I'd have to cross the street to avoid a diner we liked or something like that. We had our Sunday phone calls, always at three o'clock in the afternoon, right after she finished her Sunday crossword puzzle. I'd sit in my brown leather recliner, the one by the window in the den, and we'd talk for an hour or so about nothing in particular. The weather, the grandkids, whatever was on the news. So I suppose that recliner reminds me of her more than anything else, but I sit in it all day long anyway. I certainly don't avoid it.\n\nIt's not like I'm hiding from her memory. You can't really hide from it anyway, can you? When you lose someone you've known your entire life, someone who was there from the very beginning, the memory of them is just... it's just a part of the air you breathe. It's in the quiet moments. It's wrapped up in that heavy, hollow feeling I described to you earlier. I don't need a specific trigger, like a photograph or a certain song on the radio or a specific person, to bring it all to the surface. It's just always there, pressing down on me, whether my eyes are open or closed. \n\nSo, no, I'm not staying away from Pastor Miller or the youth program because they make me think of my sister. I'm staying away because... well, because I'm just so utterly depleted. Like I was saying, I just don't have the fortitude to put on a brave face, to make small talk, to pretend I'm capable of functioning when I'm so clearly not. It's the sheer exhaustion of it all, the absolute lack of mental energy, not the memories themselves, that keeps me shut away in the house. The memories are going to find me wherever I am, so there's really no point in trying to outrun them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, absolutely. I think I've touched on that a bit already, but it's really quite profound. The things that used to bring me joy, that used to fill up my days and give me a sense of accomplishment... they just hold absolutely no appeal for me anymore. It's like the color has been drained right out of them. \n\nTake my woodworking, for example. I mentioned the youth program earlier, but even just my own personal projects... I have this beautiful piece of cherry wood sitting on my workbench right now. I bought it months ago, back in the fall, intending to make a new mantle clock for the living room. I had the design all drawn out, I had the gears ordered... but I haven't even touched those wood pieces on my bench. They're just gathering dust. I look at them, and I know I should want to work on them, I know I used to love the smell of the sawdust and the feel of the wood taking shape under my hands, but now... nothing. Just that heavy, hollow feeling I told you about.\n\nAnd it's not just the solitary hobbies, either. It's the social ones, too. I've been involved with the choir planning committee for years, helping select the hymns for the Sunday services, organizing the sheet music... I haven't been to a planning meeting in I don't know how long. I just skipped them entirely. And my Tuesday night chess games with Arthur down at the community center... we've been playing every Tuesday for the better part of a decade. We'd play three games, have a cup of terrible instant coffee, and argue about local politics. But I haven't been down there in weeks. I just can't muster the energy to care about the board, or the conversation, or any of it. \n\nEven fishing... my goodness, I've been a fisherman my whole life. My father taught me when I was just a boy, standing on the banks of the river with a simple cane pole. I have a whole collection of rods and reels in the garage, all meticulously maintained. Spring is usually my favorite time to get out on the water, when the crappie are biting. But right now? The very idea of hooking up the trailer, driving down to the lake, getting the boat in the water... it just sounds like so much more effort than it's worth. The thought of sitting out there on the water, which used to be the most peaceful thing in the world to me, just feels exhausting. And that... well, that is just not like me at all. It's like I'm a completely different person, someone who just doesn't care about the things that used to define him.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your sister's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well... no. No, I don't think I feel guilty. I mean, it's natural, isn't it, when someone passes away, to turn everything over and over in your mind, to scrutinize every little interaction? You always have that fleeting thought of whether you should have made the drive up there more often, instead of just relying on those Sunday phone calls I told you about. But... well, the drive had gotten so hard on my back these last few years, and she completely understood that. We were always very practical with each other, right from when we were kids. \n\nShe had been declining for a little while, you see. It wasn't a sudden, shocking accident where things were left unsaid or where we parted on bad terms. We had said everything we needed to say. We knew exactly where we stood with one another. We were close, as close as a brother and sister can be when they live a couple of states apart. I always made sure to send her those little care packages she liked—mostly just the local preserves from the farmer's market here, because she had an absolute weakness for blackberry jam—and she knew I loved her. \n\nSo, no, I don't have that terrible burden of \"what if\" or \"I should have.\" I don't feel like I failed her in any way, or that I could have somehow prevented it. The doctors did everything they could, and at our age... well, you know how it goes. The machinery just starts to wear down eventually. I suppose if we had been estranged, or if we had had some big falling out years ago over a family inheritance or some silly misunderstanding, I might be sitting here wracked with guilt. But there was absolutely none of that. Just a good, steady, lifelong relationship. \n\nSo the pain I'm feeling, this absolute crushing weight... it's not guilt. It's just the sheer, overwhelming loss of her. It's the empty space she left behind. The fact that the phone isn't going to ring at three o'clock on Sunday anymore. I just... I just miss her terribly. There's no guilt tangled up in it at all, just an unbearable amount of missing her.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus your attention on things?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, that's actually a big part of why I finally decided I needed to come in and see someone. My mind just... it will not hold onto things anymore. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing, and it really frightened me when I first started noticing it. \n\nJust the other day, for instance... I know I told you I've been staying away from the youth maker program down at the community center, but I still felt obligated to at least send them a note, you know? Just to pass along some instructions for a project they were starting, some basic measurements for the wood scraps. Something I have done a hundred times. Something I could absolutely do in my sleep before. \n\nSo, I sit down at the kitchen table. I get out my yellow legal pad and a pen, and I try to write this simple note. And... I just couldn't do it. The numbers and the words, they just scatter on me. It's like they literally float away the second I try to pin them down on the paper. I'd write one sentence, and then halfway through the next one, I'd completely lose my train of thought. I'd stare at the page, and it was just a jumble. I couldn't remember if I had already written down the dimensions, I couldn't hold the sequence of steps in my head for more than five seconds... it's just this thick, impenetrable fog. \n\nI must have sat there for forty-five minutes trying to write three short paragraphs, just staring at the lines on the paper, trying to force my brain to focus. But it wouldn't. It's like my mind just refuses to grip the gears anymore, if that makes sense. I've always prided myself on being sharp, on keeping things organized in my head, but right now... I can't even hold onto a single thought long enough to write it down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Just that heavy, hollow feeling I told you about.\n\nAnd it's not just the solitary hobbies, either. It's the social ones, too. I've been involved with the choir planning committee for years, helping select the hymns for the Sunday services, organizing the sheet music... I haven't been to a planning meeting in I don't know how long. I just skipped them entirely. And my Tuesday night chess games with Arthur down at the community center... we've been playing every Tuesday for the better part of a decade. We'd play three games, have a cup of terrible instant coffee, and argue about local politics. But I haven't been down there in weeks. I just can't muster the energy to care about the board, or the conversation, or any of it. \n\nEven fishing... my goodness, I've been a fisherman my whole life. My father taught me when I was just a boy, standing on the banks of the river with a simple cane pole. I have a whole collection of rods and reels in the garage, all meticulously maintained. Spring is usually my favorite time to get out on the water, when the crappie are biting. But right now? The very idea of hooking up the trailer, driving down to the lake, getting the boat in the water... it just sounds like so much more effort than it's worth. The thought of sitting out there on the water, which used to be the most peaceful thing in the world to me, just feels exhausting. And that... well, that is just not like me at all. It's like I'm a completely different person, someone who just doesn't care about the things that used to define him.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your sister's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well... no. No, I don't think I feel guilty. I mean, it's natural, isn't it, when someone passes away, to turn everything over and over in your mind, to scrutinize every little interaction? You always have that fleeting thought of whether you should have made the drive up there more often, instead of just relying on those Sunday phone calls I told you about. But... well, the drive had gotten so hard on my back these last few years, and she completely understood that. We were always very practical with each other, right from when we were kids. \n\nShe had been declining for a little while, you see. It wasn't a sudden, shocking accident where things were left unsaid or where we parted on bad terms. We had said everything we needed to say. We knew exactly where we stood with one another. We were close, as close as a brother and sister can be when they live a couple of states apart. I always made sure to send her those little care packages she liked—mostly just the local preserves from the farmer's market here, because she had an absolute weakness for blackberry jam—and she knew I loved her. \n\nSo, no, I don't have that terrible burden of \"what if\" or \"I should have.\" I don't feel like I failed her in any way, or that I could have somehow prevented it. The doctors did everything they could, and at our age... well, you know how it goes. The machinery just starts to wear down eventually. I suppose if we had been estranged, or if we had had some big falling out years ago over a family inheritance or some silly misunderstanding, I might be sitting here wracked with guilt. But there was absolutely none of that. Just a good, steady, lifelong relationship. \n\nSo the pain I'm feeling, this absolute crushing weight... it's not guilt. It's just the sheer, overwhelming loss of her. It's the empty space she left behind. The fact that the phone isn't going to ring at three o'clock on Sunday anymore. I just... I just miss her terribly. There's no guilt tangled up in it at all, just an unbearable amount of missing her.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus your attention on things?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, that's actually a big part of why I finally decided I needed to come in and see someone. My mind just... it will not hold onto things anymore. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing, and it really frightened me when I first started noticing it. \n\nJust the other day, for instance... I know I told you I've been staying away from the youth maker program down at the community center, but I still felt obligated to at least send them a note, you know? Just to pass along some instructions for a project they were starting, some basic measurements for the wood scraps. Something I have done a hundred times. Something I could absolutely do in my sleep before. \n\nSo, I sit down at the kitchen table. I get out my yellow legal pad and a pen, and I try to write this simple note. And... I just couldn't do it. The numbers and the words, they just scatter on me. It's like they literally float away the second I try to pin them down on the paper. I'd write one sentence, and then halfway through the next one, I'd completely lose my train of thought. I'd stare at the page, and it was just a jumble. I couldn't remember if I had already written down the dimensions, I couldn't hold the sequence of steps in my head for more than five seconds... it's just this thick, impenetrable fog. \n\nI must have sat there for forty-five minutes trying to write three short paragraphs, just staring at the lines on the paper, trying to force my brain to focus. But it wouldn't. It's like my mind just refuses to grip the gears anymore, if that makes sense. I've always prided myself on being sharp, on keeping things organized in my head, but right now... I can't even hold onto a single thought long enough to write it down.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your appetite since your sister passed away?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"Food has mostly lost its appeal, so I push breakfast around the plate and by supper my wife is reminding me I have barely eaten anything all day.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... yes. Yes, there has been a very noticeable change there, too. It’s funny you ask, because my wife and I were just... well, she was just mentioning it to me yesterday. Food has just completely, entirely lost its appeal. It’s almost exactly like what I was saying about the woodworking and the fishing—that same sort of gray, heavy apathy has just bled right over into my eating habits. 

I’ve always been a man who enjoyed a good meal, you know? For forty years, I’ve started my day with a proper, hearty breakfast. But now... I sit down at the kitchen table in the morning, and I just look at it. I take my fork and I just sort of push my breakfast around the plate. I might move the eggs to one side, maybe break off a tiny corner of toast, but the thought of actually putting it in my mouth and chewing it... it just feels like an insurmountable chore. It all tastes like sawdust to me anyway. 

So I don't eat it. And then the day just stretches on, and I don't even think about lunch. The hours go by. My stomach doesn't rumble, I don't get cravings for a sandwich or an apple or anything at all. I just sit there in the house, lost in that fog I told you about. 

And then, before I know it, it's evening, and my wife is setting a plate of supper down in front of me. She's a wonderful cook, always has been, but she'll set down this lovely meal and she'll have to look at me—with this very worried expression she gets lately—and remind me that I need to eat, because I have barely eaten a single thing all day long. She'll say, "You didn't touch your breakfast, and you skipped lunch entirely, you have to try to eat this." And I know she's right, I know a man my age needs sustenance to keep the engine running, but even with a hot dinner sitting right under my nose, I just can't muster any interest in it whatsoever. The desire is just completely gone.

But it wouldn't. It's like my mind just refuses to grip the gears anymore, if that makes sense. I've always prided myself on being sharp, on keeping things organized in my head, but right now... I can't even hold onto a single thought long enough to write it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Food has just completely, entirely lost its appeal. It’s almost exactly like what I was saying about the woodworking and the fishing—that same sort of gray, heavy apathy has just bled right over into my eating habits. \n\nI’ve always been a man who enjoyed a good meal, you know? For forty years, I’ve started my day with a proper, hearty breakfast. But now... I sit down at the kitchen table in the morning, and I just look at it. I take my fork and I just sort of push my breakfast around the plate. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, absolutely. I mean, it goes right back to what I was just telling you a moment ago, doesn't it? When food has mostly lost its appeal entirely, the weight is bound to just fall right off you. Like I said, I sit there and just push my breakfast around the plate, maybe moving a piece of toast from one side to the other, and by the time supper rolls around and my wife is reminding me that I have barely eaten a single thing all day... well, your body just stops having any fuel to run on, doesn't it? 

So, yes, the weight has dropped significantly. I've had to pull my everyday belt in by two whole notches, which I haven't had to do since I was a young man fresh out of college. Just the other morning, I was getting dressed—trying to get dressed, anyway, it takes me so much longer to just get the buttons done these days—and I put on this pair of navy slacks I’ve worn for years, and they practically slid right down my hips. They were bunching up all around the waist like a deflated balloon. 

My wife actually made me step on the bathroom scale this past weekend because she was so alarmed by how my shirts are hanging off my shoulders, and how my collarbones are sticking out. I didn't want to look at the numbers, to be perfectly honest with you, because it just makes it all feel so real, this... this wasting away. But she looked, and she just let out this heavy sigh. 

It's just simple mathematics, I suppose. If you aren't taking anything in, if every single meal is just an exercise in staring at a plate until your spouse has to coax you to take a bite, you're going to shrink. I haven't been this light in forty years. So, yes, I've lost quite a bit of weight. It's hard not to when the mere thought of chewing and swallowing feels like climbing a mountain.

It hasn't been a slow, gradual thing at all. It's been about three weeks. Just about three weeks now. That's... well, that's when my older sister passed away. \n\nWe were very close, you see. She was really my sounding board for everything, the one who always kept the family connected. And ever since she died, it's like a switch just flipped in my head. Just names of people I've known for decades who had been at the funeral. And I couldn't do it. I don't know if it's the shock of it all, or what, but it's been three weeks of my brain completely stalling out on me and it's just exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, I have. It's... it's a very particular kind of sadness, though. Not just the normal crying or missing someone, though of course I do miss her terribly. But it's more like... well, almost every single day, I wake up and before I even open my eyes, there's this physical sensation. It's this heavy, hollow feeling right here in the center of my chest. Like someone scooped out everything inside and just poured lead in there instead. \n\nAnd it just sits there, all day long. It colors absolutely everything. I mean, my wife, she's been a saint through all this. She's been so patient, trying to comfort me, bringing me tea, talking to me in that gentle, kind voice of hers. She'll sit with me on the porch and try to talk about the neighbors or the garden, just trying to bring me back to the world, I think. And I can see her lips moving, I can hear the kindness in her voice, but it's like... it's like I'm sitting at the very bottom of a deep, dark well, and she's way up at the top, calling down to me. The warmth of what she's saying just doesn't reach me down there. I can't feel it. I just sit there with that heavy emptiness, looking up at the light but completely unable to climb out. It's just this constant, overwhelming sadness that I can't seem to shake, no matter how hard I try or how much support I have around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble following conversations or train of thought in your daily interactions?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, exactly. That's... that's exactly what I was trying to explain earlier about the note for the kids. It's not just writing, it's conversations, it's my own train of thought... it all just evaporates. \n\nLike I was saying before, I sit down to do something so simple, like writing that little instruction note for the youth maker program. I mean, I've been doing that program for a while now, and before I retired, I managed complex projects, spreadsheets, endless meetings where I had to track a dozen different moving parts. I'm a college-educated man, I've always prided myself on my mental sharpness. But now? I sit there with the pen in my hand, and I think, \"Okay, I need to tell them to cut the balsa wood at a 45-degree angle.\" And before the ink even touches the paper, the thought is just... gone. It just slips away. I find myself nodding along, but inside, I'm frantically trying to grab onto the pieces of the conversation, like trying to catch leaves blowing in the wind. The numbers, the words, the plans... they just scatter on me. It's like my mind just refuses to hold onto anything anymore, whether I'm trying to write it down or just listen to my own wife speak. It's so incredibly frustrating, doctor, to feel your own mind just refusing to work the way it always has.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disoriented or unsure of what time or day it is?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I know what day it is. I know it's Tuesday, I know it's morning, I know I'm sitting here in your office. It's not like I'm waking up thinking it's 1995 or anything like that. \n\nMy wife actually asked me something similar the other day. She came in while I was sitting in the armchair—the green one by the window, where I've been spending most of my time lately—and she asked if I knew what time we were supposed to leave for the pharmacy. And I knew it was 2:30. I knew it was Thursday. I just... I didn't care. Or, well, not that I didn't care, but it just felt like such an insurmountable task to get up and go. But the actual awareness of time and place? No, that's all still intact. \n\nIt's just the contents of my thoughts that are scrambled, you know? The dates on the calendar, the clock on the wall... those are fixed. Those stay still. It's everything else that's swirling around. I always know where I am and when it is. I just... I just don't know what to do with myself once I'm there. It's like I'm anchored to the calendar perfectly fine, but the boat itself is just filling with water. Does that make sense? I'm not confused about the day, I'm just... I'm just so bogged down in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering recent events or learning new information?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not so much that I forget things that just happened. I mean, if my wife tells me she's making chicken for dinner, I know we're having chicken. I don't forget that she said it. And I remember her bringing me my tea this morning, in the blue mug, the one our daughter bought us on her trip to Maine. I remember the steam rising off it and how it warmed my hands, even if it didn't do much to warm me on the inside. \n\nSo no, it's not like my memory is wiped clean of what happened yesterday or this morning. It's more about... well, like I was saying before, it's the holding onto the threads of things as they're happening. Learning new things? Gosh, I haven't even tried to learn anything new since... since my sister passed. The very thought of trying to sit down and read a book or figure out a new recipe or, heaven forbid, try to learn a new computer program or something... it just feels impossible. Not because my brain can't store the information, but because I can't even gather enough focus to let the information in in the first place. \n\nIt's like... imagine trying to fill a bucket with water, but the bucket has a lid on it. It's not that the bucket is broken or has holes in it, it's just that the water just splashes right off the top. That's how I feel when I try to take in anything new right now. The lid is just shut tight with all this... this heaviness. So I wouldn't say I have amnesia or anything like that. I remember the events of the day perfectly fine. I just... I just don't have the energy or the mental space to absorb anything new or complicated. It's all just too much effort. Everything is just too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble performing your daily routines or tasks due to these cognitive difficulties?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I am. And it's... it's a terrible feeling, to be honest with you. It's not just the note I was telling you about earlier, the one for the youth maker program. It's the whole program. I've had to just... let it go. I've been involved with those kids for years, teaching them how to build things, how to use their hands, how to take pride in a project from start to finish. And now? I can't even walk into my own workshop. \n\nMy wife and my pastor, Pastor Miller—he's a good man, he's known me for decades—they actually had to step in and take over because... well, because I just don't feel safe being out there by myself anymore. The tools, the machinery, the saws... when your mind is as scattered as mine is right now, when you can't hold onto a thought long enough to write a simple sentence, being around a table saw is a recipe for disaster. I just stare at the equipment and feel this overwhelming sense of dread, like I'm going to make a mistake that I can't undo. So I've had to completely step away from the program. \n\nAnd it's not just the maker program, either. It's bleeding into everything. I've been on the church property committee since, gosh, since the late nineties. I was always the one organizing the spring clean-up, making sure the gutters were cleared before the winter, coordinating the volunteers. I had spreadsheets for all of it. But I haven't been to a committee meeting in months. I just stopped going. I couldn't face the thought of sitting there, trying to follow the agenda, trying to make decisions about budgets and repairs when I can barely decide whether to get out of my chair in the morning. \n\nEven my garden... I've always loved my garden. Tomatoes, zucchini, a whole row of sunflowers that my grandchildren love to measure themselves against in the late summer. I usually start my seeds indoors in February, getting everything ready for the spring thaw. But this year? The seed packets are still sitting in a shoebox in the mudroom, unopened. The weeds are probably taking over the raised beds by now, but I haven't even gone out to look. I just sit in my chair and look out the window, and I know I should go out there, I know I should pull the weeds and turn the soil, but the thought of actually doing it... it's like trying to move a mountain. I just can't. So, yes, my daily routines, the things that used to make up my life, the things that gave me purpose and structure... they've all just fallen away. I'm not doing any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to experience a full range of emotions, or do you feel numb to things that would normally affect you?\n\nPatient: Numb... I don't know if numb is exactly the right word for it, because numb makes it sound like there's nothing there at all. Like novocaine at the dentist. But for me, it's not an absence of feeling, it's... well, it's the presence of something else that just blocks everything normal out. \n\nMost days, the very second I open my eyes—before I even fully register the sunlight coming through the blinds or hear the birds outside—I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling right in the center of my chest. It's a strange contradiction, isn't it? To feel so incredibly heavy and yet completely hollow at the exact same time. But it's like a lead weight that's just empty inside, pressing down on my ribs before I've even thrown off the covers. \n\nAnd because that feeling is so huge, so all-consuming, there just isn't room for anything else. No joy, no excitement, not even regular sadness. Just that heavy ache. And it puts this... this terrible distance between me and the rest of the world. Even with my wife. She's been so incredibly patient through all of this, more than I deserve, really. She'll come into the room, and she'll talk so kindly to me, speaking softly, trying to offer some comfort or just tell me about her morning. A husband should feel that, right? He should feel the warmth of his wife's voice. \n\nBut when she's standing right there, being so gentle and loving... I just feel like I'm sitting at the very bottom of a deep, dark well. I can look up and see the little circle of light at the top, and I can hear her voice echoing down to me, but she's so far away. The kindness just doesn't reach me down there in the dark. I know in my head that she loves me, but I can't feel the comfort of it. I can't feel the connection. I'm just stuck down at the bottom of the well with that hollow weight in my chest. So, no, I'm definitely not experiencing a full range of emotions. It's just that one, constant, heavy emptiness swallowing up everything else that tries to get in.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain people, places, or activities because they remind you of your sister?\n\nPatient: Well... no. No, I wouldn't say that's it. I mean, it's true that I'm avoiding practically everything and everyone right now, like I told you about the workshop and the garden and the church committee. But it's not... it's not because those things specifically remind me of her. \n\nShe lived a couple of states away, you see. So it's not as if my daily routine around town is full of places we used to go together, where I'd have to cross the street to avoid a diner we liked or something like that. We had our Sunday phone calls, always at three o'clock in the afternoon, right after she finished her Sunday crossword puzzle. I'd sit in my brown leather recliner, the one by the window in the den, and we'd talk for an hour or so about nothing in particular. The weather, the grandkids, whatever was on the news. So I suppose that recliner reminds me of her more than anything else, but I sit in it all day long anyway. I certainly don't avoid it.\n\nIt's not like I'm hiding from her memory. You can't really hide from it anyway, can you? When you lose someone you've known your entire life, someone who was there from the very beginning, the memory of them is just... it's just a part of the air you breathe. It's in the quiet moments. It's wrapped up in that heavy, hollow feeling I described to you earlier. I don't need a specific trigger, like a photograph or a certain song on the radio or a specific person, to bring it all to the surface. It's just always there, pressing down on me, whether my eyes are open or closed. \n\nSo, no, I'm not staying away from Pastor Miller or the youth program because they make me think of my sister. I'm staying away because... well, because I'm just so utterly depleted. Like I was saying, I just don't have the fortitude to put on a brave face, to make small talk, to pretend I'm capable of functioning when I'm so clearly not. It's the sheer exhaustion of it all, the absolute lack of mental energy, not the memories themselves, that keeps me shut away in the house. The memories are going to find me wherever I am, so there's really no point in trying to outrun them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, absolutely. I think I've touched on that a bit already, but it's really quite profound. The things that used to bring me joy, that used to fill up my days and give me a sense of accomplishment... they just hold absolutely no appeal for me anymore. It's like the color has been drained right out of them. \n\nTake my woodworking, for example. I mentioned the youth program earlier, but even just my own personal projects... I have this beautiful piece of cherry wood sitting on my workbench right now. I bought it months ago, back in the fall, intending to make a new mantle clock for the living room. I had the design all drawn out, I had the gears ordered... but I haven't even touched those wood pieces on my bench. They're just gathering dust. I look at them, and I know I should want to work on them, I know I used to love the smell of the sawdust and the feel of the wood taking shape under my hands, but now... nothing. Just that heavy, hollow feeling I told you about.\n\nAnd it's not just the solitary hobbies, either. It's the social ones, too. I've been involved with the choir planning committee for years, helping select the hymns for the Sunday services, organizing the sheet music... I haven't been to a planning meeting in I don't know how long. I just skipped them entirely. And my Tuesday night chess games with Arthur down at the community center... we've been playing every Tuesday for the better part of a decade. We'd play three games, have a cup of terrible instant coffee, and argue about local politics. But I haven't been down there in weeks. I just can't muster the energy to care about the board, or the conversation, or any of it. \n\nEven fishing... my goodness, I've been a fisherman my whole life. My father taught me when I was just a boy, standing on the banks of the river with a simple cane pole. I have a whole collection of rods and reels in the garage, all meticulously maintained. Spring is usually my favorite time to get out on the water, when the crappie are biting. But right now? The very idea of hooking up the trailer, driving down to the lake, getting the boat in the water... it just sounds like so much more effort than it's worth. The thought of sitting out there on the water, which used to be the most peaceful thing in the world to me, just feels exhausting. And that... well, that is just not like me at all. It's like I'm a completely different person, someone who just doesn't care about the things that used to define him.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your sister's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well... no. No, I don't think I feel guilty. I mean, it's natural, isn't it, when someone passes away, to turn everything over and over in your mind, to scrutinize every little interaction? You always have that fleeting thought of whether you should have made the drive up there more often, instead of just relying on those Sunday phone calls I told you about. We were close, as close as a brother and sister can be when they live a couple of states apart. I always made sure to send her those little care packages she liked—mostly just the local preserves from the farmer's market here, because she had an absolute weakness for blackberry jam—and she knew I loved her. \n\nSo, no, I don't have that terrible burden of \"what if\" or \"I should have.\" I don't feel like I failed her in any way, or that I could have somehow prevented it. The doctors did everything they could, and at our age... well, you know how it goes. The machinery just starts to wear down eventually. I suppose if we had been estranged, or if we had had some big falling out years ago over a family inheritance or some silly misunderstanding, I might be sitting here wracked with guilt. But there was absolutely none of that. Just a good, steady, lifelong relationship. \n\nSo the pain I'm feeling, this absolute crushing weight... it's not guilt. It's just the sheer, overwhelming loss of her. It's the empty space she left behind. The fact that the phone isn't going to ring at three o'clock on Sunday anymore. I just... I just miss her terribly. There's no guilt tangled up in it at all, just an unbearable amount of missing her.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus your attention on things?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, that's actually a big part of why I finally decided I needed to come in and see someone. My mind just... it will not hold onto things anymore. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing, and it really frightened me when I first started noticing it. \n\nJust the other day, for instance... I know I told you I've been staying away from the youth maker program down at the community center, but I still felt obligated to at least send them a note, you know? Just to pass along some instructions for a project they were starting, some basic measurements for the wood scraps. Something I have done a hundred times. Something I could absolutely do in my sleep before. \n\nSo, I sit down at the kitchen table. I get out my yellow legal pad and a pen, and I try to write this simple note. And... I just couldn't do it. The numbers and the words, they just scatter on me. It's like they literally float away the second I try to pin them down on the paper. I'd write one sentence, and then halfway through the next one, I'd completely lose my train of thought. I'd stare at the page, and it was just a jumble. I couldn't remember if I had already written down the dimensions, I couldn't hold the sequence of steps in my head for more than five seconds... it's just this thick, impenetrable fog. \n\nI must have sat there for forty-five minutes trying to write three short paragraphs, just staring at the lines on the paper, trying to force my brain to focus. But it wouldn't. It's like my mind just refuses to grip the gears anymore, if that makes sense. I've always prided myself on being sharp, on keeping things organized in my head, but right now... I can't even hold onto a single thought long enough to write it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, there has been a very noticeable change there, too. It’s funny you ask, because my wife and I were just... well, she was just mentioning it to me yesterday. Food has just completely, entirely lost its appeal. It’s almost exactly like what I was saying about the woodworking and the fishing—that same sort of gray, heavy apathy has just bled right over into my eating habits. \n\nI’ve always been a man who enjoyed a good meal, you know? For forty years, I’ve started my day with a proper, hearty breakfast. But now... I sit down at the kitchen table in the morning, and I just look at it. I take my fork and I just sort of push my breakfast around the plate. I might move the eggs to one side, maybe break off a tiny corner of toast, but the thought of actually putting it in my mouth and chewing it... it just feels like an insurmountable chore. It all tastes like sawdust to me anyway. \n\nSo I don't eat it. And then the day just stretches on, and I don't even think about lunch. The hours go by. My stomach doesn't rumble, I don't get cravings for a sandwich or an apple or anything at all. I just sit there in the house, lost in that fog I told you about. \n\nAnd then, before I know it, it's evening, and my wife is setting a plate of supper down in front of me. She's a wonderful cook, always has been, but she'll set down this lovely meal and she'll have to look at me—with this very worried expression she gets lately—and remind me that I need to eat, because I have barely eaten a single thing all day long. She'll say, \"You didn't touch your breakfast, and you skipped lunch entirely, you have to try to eat this.\" And I know she's right, I know a man my age needs sustenance to keep the engine running, but even with a hot dinner sitting right under my nose, I just can't muster any interest in it whatsoever. The desire is just completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. I mean, it goes right back to what I was just telling you a moment ago, doesn't it? When food has mostly lost its appeal entirely, the weight is bound to just fall right off you. Like I said, I sit there and just push my breakfast around the plate, maybe moving a piece of toast from one side to the other, and by the time supper rolls around and my wife is reminding me that I have barely eaten a single thing all day... well, your body just stops having any fuel to run on, doesn't it? \n\nSo, yes, the weight has dropped significantly. I've had to pull my everyday belt in by two whole notches, which I haven't had to do since I was a young man fresh out of college. Just the other morning, I was getting dressed—trying to get dressed, anyway, it takes me so much longer to just get the buttons done these days—and I put on this pair of navy slacks I’ve worn for years, and they practically slid right down my hips. They were bunching up all around the waist like a deflated balloon. \n\nMy wife actually made me step on the bathroom scale this past weekend because she was so alarmed by how my shirts are hanging off my shoulders, and how my collarbones are sticking out. I didn't want to look at the numbers, to be perfectly honest with you, because it just makes it all feel so real, this... this wasting away. But she looked, and she just let out this heavy sigh. \n\nIt's just simple mathematics, I suppose. If you aren't taking anything in, if every single meal is just an exercise in staring at a plate until your spouse has to coax you to take a bite, you're going to shrink. I haven't been this light in forty years. So, yes, I've lost quite a bit of weight. I look at them, and I know I should want to work on them, I know I used to love the smell of the sawdust and the feel of the wood taking shape under my hands, but now... nothing. Just that heavy, hollow feeling I told you about.\n\nAnd it's not just the solitary hobbies, either. It's the social ones, too. I've been involved with the choir planning committee for years, helping select the hymns for the Sunday services, organizing the sheet music... I haven't been to a planning meeting in I don't know how long. I just skipped them entirely. And my Tuesday night chess games with Arthur down at the community center... we've been playing every Tuesday for the better part of a decade. We'd play three games, have a cup of terrible instant coffee, and argue about local politics. But I haven't been down there in weeks. I just can't muster the energy to care about the board, or the conversation, or any of it. \n\nEven fishing... my goodness, I've been a fisherman my whole life. My father taught me when I was just a boy, standing on the banks of the river with a simple cane pole. I have a whole collection of rods and reels in the garage, all meticulously maintained. Spring is usually my favorite time to get out on the water, when the crappie are biting. But right now? The very idea of hooking up the trailer, driving down to the lake, getting the boat in the water... it just sounds like so much more effort than it's worth. The thought of sitting out there on the water, which used to be the most peaceful thing in the world to me, just feels exhausting. And that... well, that is just not like me at all. It's like I'm a completely different person, someone who just doesn't care about the things that used to define him.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your sister's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well... no. No, I don't think I feel guilty. I mean, it's natural, isn't it, when someone passes away, to turn everything over and over in your mind, to scrutinize every little interaction? You always have that fleeting thought of whether you should have made the drive up there more often, instead of just relying on those Sunday phone calls I told you about. But... well, the drive had gotten so hard on my back these last few years, and she completely understood that. We were always very practical with each other, right from when we were kids. \n\nShe had been declining for a little while, you see. It wasn't a sudden, shocking accident where things were left unsaid or where we parted on bad terms. We had said everything we needed to say. We knew exactly where we stood with one another. We were close, as close as a brother and sister can be when they live a couple of states apart. I always made sure to send her those little care packages she liked—mostly just the local preserves from the farmer's market here, because she had an absolute weakness for blackberry jam—and she knew I loved her. \n\nSo, no, I don't have that terrible burden of \"what if\" or \"I should have.\" I don't feel like I failed her in any way, or that I could have somehow prevented it. The doctors did everything they could, and at our age... well, you know how it goes. The machinery just starts to wear down eventually. I suppose if we had been estranged, or if we had had some big falling out years ago over a family inheritance or some silly misunderstanding, I might be sitting here wracked with guilt. But there was absolutely none of that. Just a good, steady, lifelong relationship. \n\nSo the pain I'm feeling, this absolute crushing weight... it's not guilt. It's just the sheer, overwhelming loss of her. It's the empty space she left behind. The fact that the phone isn't going to ring at three o'clock on Sunday anymore. I just... I just miss her terribly. There's no guilt tangled up in it at all, just an unbearable amount of missing her.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus your attention on things?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, that's actually a big part of why I finally decided I needed to come in and see someone. My mind just... it will not hold onto things anymore. It's the strangest, most frustrating thing, and it really frightened me when I first started noticing it. \n\nJust the other day, for instance... I know I told you I've been staying away from the youth maker program down at the community center, but I still felt obligated to at least send them a note, you know? Just to pass along some instructions for a project they were starting, some basic measurements for the wood scraps. Something I have done a hundred times. Something I could absolutely do in my sleep before. \n\nSo, I sit down at the kitchen table. I get out my yellow legal pad and a pen, and I try to write this simple note. And... I just couldn't do it. The numbers and the words, they just scatter on me. It's like they literally float away the second I try to pin them down on the paper. I'd write one sentence, and then halfway through the next one, I'd completely lose my train of thought. I'd stare at the page, and it was just a jumble. I couldn't remember if I had already written down the dimensions, I couldn't hold the sequence of steps in my head for more than five seconds... it's just this thick, impenetrable fog. \n\nI must have sat there for forty-five minutes trying to write three short paragraphs, just staring at the lines on the paper, trying to force my brain to focus. But it wouldn't. It's like my mind just refuses to grip the gears anymore, if that makes sense. I've always prided myself on being sharp, on keeping things organized in my head, but right now... I can't even hold onto a single thought long enough to write it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, there has been a very noticeable change there, too. It’s funny you ask, because my wife and I were just... well, she was just mentioning it to me yesterday. Food has just completely, entirely lost its appeal. It’s almost exactly like what I was saying about the woodworking and the fishing—that same sort of gray, heavy apathy has just bled right over into my eating habits. \n\nI’ve always been a man who enjoyed a good meal, you know? For forty years, I’ve started my day with a proper, hearty breakfast. But now... I sit down at the kitchen table in the morning, and I just look at it. I take my fork and I just sort of push my breakfast around the plate. I might move the eggs to one side, maybe break off a tiny corner of toast, but the thought of actually putting it in my mouth and chewing it... it just feels like an insurmountable chore. It all tastes like sawdust to me anyway. \n\nSo I don't eat it. And then the day just stretches on, and I don't even think about lunch. The hours go by. My stomach doesn't rumble, I don't get cravings for a sandwich or an apple or anything at all. I just sit there in the house, lost in that fog I told you about. \n\nAnd then, before I know it, it's evening, and my wife is setting a plate of supper down in front of me. She's a wonderful cook, always has been, but she'll set down this lovely meal and she'll have to look at me—with this very worried expression she gets lately—and remind me that I need to eat, because I have barely eaten a single thing all day long. She'll say, \"You didn't touch your breakfast, and you skipped lunch entirely, you have to try to eat this.\" And I know she's right, I know a man my age needs sustenance to keep the engine running, but even with a hot dinner sitting right under my nose, I just can't muster any interest in it whatsoever. The desire is just completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. I mean, it goes right back to what I was just telling you a moment ago, doesn't it? When food has mostly lost its appeal entirely, the weight is bound to just fall right off you. Like I said, I sit there and just push my breakfast around the plate, maybe moving a piece of toast from one side to the other, and by the time supper rolls around and my wife is reminding me that I have barely eaten a single thing all day... well, your body just stops having any fuel to run on, doesn't it? \n\nSo, yes, the weight has dropped significantly. I've had to pull my everyday belt in by two whole notches, which I haven't had to do since I was a young man fresh out of college. Just the other morning, I was getting dressed—trying to get dressed, anyway, it takes me so much longer to just get the buttons done these days—and I put on this pair of navy slacks I’ve worn for years, and they practically slid right down my hips. They were bunching up all around the waist like a deflated balloon. \n\nMy wife actually made me step on the bathroom scale this past weekend because she was so alarmed by how my shirts are hanging off my shoulders, and how my collarbones are sticking out. I didn't want to look at the numbers, to be perfectly honest with you, because it just makes it all feel so real, this... this wasting away. But she looked, and she just let out this heavy sigh. \n\nIt's just simple mathematics, I suppose. If you aren't taking anything in, if every single meal is just an exercise in staring at a plate until your spouse has to coax you to take a bite, you're going to shrink. I haven't been this light in forty years. So, yes, I've lost quite a bit of weight. It's hard not to when the mere thought of chewing and swallowing feels like climbing a mountain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Well... yes. Yes, I suppose I have. It's... it's an awful thing to admit out loud, really, especially to a stranger, but since you're asking directly, I feel I ought to be honest about it. It's not something I ever thought I'd struggle with. I've always been a steady sort of man, you know? A provider. Someone people could lean on. But since she passed, everything has just... tilted. 

And lately, I sit in my armchair in the living room, just watching my wife. I watch her hovering around me, looking so worried all the time, trying to get me to eat, trying to get me to talk... and I can see the toll this is taking on her. I can see the exhaustion in her eyes. And I just keep thinking... I keep thinking that maybe she would be better off without me dragging her down into this heavy, gray misery. Maybe the whole family would just be relieved, eventually, if they didn't have to worry about this hollowed-out version of me anymore. It's this dark, persistent thought that just sits there in the back of my mind, whispering that I'm nothing but a burden now.

And... well, there's the workshop, too. My woodworking shop out in the garage. I told you how much I used to love it out there, but now... I haven't been able to go in there for weeks. Not really. I'll walk out to the garage to get something, maybe a screwdriver or a roll of tape for my wife, and I'll look over at the workbench. I'll look at the tools. The saws, the chisels, the heavy equipment... things I've used safely and happily for decades. 

But now, when I look at them, I get this... this cold feeling in the pit of my stomach. Because my mind starts to wander. It starts to go to these dark, frightening places. I start thinking about what I could do with those tools if I just... if I just wanted all of this to stop. The fog, the exhaustion, the feeling of being completely useless. And it scares me. It genuinely terrifies me, doctor. I get so scared of where my thoughts could go if I were left alone out there for too long, with all those sharp edges and all that quiet. So I just turn around and walk back into the house, and I lock the door behind me. I just don't trust myself around it anymore, and that... that might be the hardest part of all this to swallow.
